========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not always like I’m worrying about a specific thing, like a deadline or an email. It’s more like I’m scanning everything.

On the train I’ll notice where the exits are before I even sit down, and in meetings I’m watching who’s near the door or who’s standing too close. Even at lunch with my team, when we’re supposed to just be talking about whatever—someone’s dog, or this place near the office that has really good noodles—I’m still half-listening to noises in the hallway instead of the conversation.

So… yes, anxious, I guess. But it feels more like being on alert all the time than normal worrying.

Yeah… it’s hard to shut off, even when I know there isn’t one clear thing I’m supposed to be worried about.

Yeah, I do. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, I can’t just switch it off.

Like on the train, I’ll be thinking about something totally normal—what I need to do at work, or whether I forgot to reply to an email—and then suddenly I’ve already checked where the exits are. Then I get frustrated with myself because I know there’s nothing real happening, but my brain keeps doing it anyway.

I’m not sure. I mean… not in the same way, I don’t think.

Work is the thing my mind keeps coming back to, or situations that feel like they connect to that somehow. Everyday stuff like bills or groceries or calling to make an appointment—I can get annoyed or put it off, but I don’t know if I’d call it worry exactly.

Although, now that you say “variety,” I’m thinking about how I do overthink little things sometimes, like whether I sounded weird in a text, but then again I’ve always done that a bit. My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.

So… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. It feels more specific than that, but I’m not sure I’m explaining it right.

My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… mostly sleep, I think. I mean, concentration too, maybe, but the sleep is the thing I notice because then the next day everything feels harder.

Most nights I’m lying there replaying these tiny details from work, like what someone’s face looked like in a meeting or whether I missed something important. And then sometimes I’ll jolt awake from dreams where I’m hiding under the conference table again, which sounds— I don’t know, it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in the moment.

Even if I technically get six or seven hours, I wake up feeling like I never actually powered down. Like my body was lying there, but my brain was still running in the background the whole time.

On the train I’m clocking the exits before I even sit down. In meetings I notice who’s standing too close, or where the door is, and then I’m not really listening the way I should be. Even at lunch with my team, which should be totally normal, I’ll be half in the conversation and half listening to noises in the hallway.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I missed what someone said, and I feel awkward, like I’m being rude, but I can’t just… tell my brain to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop or control your anxious thoughts, even when you’re aware that they might not make sense or aren’t based on anything real?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, I can’t just switch it off.\n\nLike on the train, I’ll be thinking about something totally normal—what I need to do at work, or whether I forgot to reply to an email—and then suddenly I’ve already checked where the exits are. Or in meetings, I’m supposed to be listening, but I’m noticing who’s standing too close to me or where people are in the room.\n\nAnd lunch with my team is probably the best example because it’s not a threatening situation at all. It’s just people talking about weekend plans or whatever, and I still have one ear on the hallway noises instead of being fully in the conversation. Then I get frustrated with myself because I know there’s nothing real happening, but my brain keeps doing it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying or feeling anxious about a variety of things in your life, not just work—like everyday responsibilities, your health, or things happening in your personal life—even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… not in the same way, I don’t think.\n\nWork is the thing my mind keeps coming back to, or situations that feel like they connect to that somehow. Everyday stuff like bills or groceries or calling to make an appointment—I can get annoyed or put it off, but I don’t know if I’d call it worry exactly.\n\nAlthough, now that you say “variety,” I’m thinking about how I do overthink little things sometimes, like whether I sounded weird in a text, but then again I’ve always done that a bit. My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. It feels more specific than that, but I’m not sure I’m explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or difficulty concentrating that come along with these anxious feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly sleep, I think. I mean, concentration too, maybe, but the sleep is the thing I notice because then the next day everything feels harder.\n\nMost nights I’m lying there replaying these tiny details from work, like what someone’s face looked like in a meeting or whether I missed something important. And then sometimes I’ll jolt awake from dreams where I’m hiding under the conference table again, which sounds— I don’t know, it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in the moment.\n\nEven if I technically get six or seven hours, I wake up feeling like I never actually powered down. Yeah… yes, I think so. Not constantly like I’m panicking, but I’m kind of scanning without meaning to.

It’s weird because I know it’s supposed to be safe, like it’s just lunch, but my brain doesn’t seem to believe that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or like you’re not worth much, most days—even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “down” isn’t always the word I would use. It’s more like this feeling that something changed in me and I can’t get back to who I was.\n\nLike, I got promoted before all this, and people at work saw me as competent, the person who could handle problems. And now I keep thinking work isn’t safe anymore, even when I know that sounds dramatic. Then I start wondering if I’m… damaged somehow. Like the version of me who could just do the job and trust herself isn’t really there now.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling around it. I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, but it does make me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop your worrying, even when you know there isn’t really anything specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s hard to shut off, even when I know there isn’t one clear thing I’m supposed to be worried about.\n\nIt’s like my brain keeps checking anyway. On the train I’m clocking the exits before I even sit down. In meetings I notice who’s standing too close, or where the door is, and then I’m not really listening the way I should be. Even at lunch with my team, which should be totally normal, I’ll be half in the conversation and half listening to noises in the hallway.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I missed what someone said, and I feel awkward, like I’m being rude, but I can’t just… tell my brain to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop or control your anxious thoughts, even when you’re aware that they might not make sense or aren’t based on anything real?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, I can’t just switch it off.\n\nLike on the train, I’ll be thinking about something totally normal—what I need to do at work, or whether I forgot to reply to an email—and then suddenly I’ve already checked where the exits are. It’s just people talking about weekend plans or whatever, and I still have one ear on the hallway noises instead of being fully in the conversation. Then I get frustrated with myself because I know there’s nothing real happening, but my brain keeps doing it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying or feeling anxious about a variety of things in your life, not just work—like everyday responsibilities, your health, or things happening in your personal life—even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Everyday stuff like bills or groceries or calling to make an appointment—I can get annoyed or put it off, but I don’t know if I’d call it worry exactly.\n\nAlthough, now that you say “variety,” I’m thinking about how I do overthink little things sometimes, like whether I sounded weird in a text, but then again I’ve always done that a bit. My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. Like my body was lying there, but my brain was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about several different things in your life, like work, relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities, even when most people might not think those things are a big deal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I don’t notice it that way.\n\nLike, work stuff, yes, that gets in my head. It follows me, yeah. I wish it stayed in one place, like if I could just leave it at work when I badge out, but it’s not really like that.

Then I get frustrated with myself because I know there’s nothing real happening, but my brain keeps doing it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying or feeling anxious about a variety of things in your life, not just work—like everyday responsibilities, your health, or things happening in your personal life—even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… not in the same way, I don’t think.\n\nWork is the thing my mind keeps coming back to, or situations that feel like they connect to that somehow. Everyday stuff like bills or groceries or calling to make an appointment—I can get annoyed or put it off, but I don’t know if I’d call it worry exactly.\n\nAlthough, now that you say “variety,” I’m thinking about how I do overthink little things sometimes, like whether I sounded weird in a text, but then again I’ve always done that a bit. My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. It feels more specific than that, but I’m not sure I’m explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or difficulty concentrating that come along with these anxious feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly sleep, I think. I mean, concentration too, maybe, but the sleep is the thing I notice because then the next day everything feels harder.\n\nMost nights I’m lying there replaying these tiny details from work, like what someone’s face looked like in a meeting or whether I missed something important. Like my body was lying there, but my brain was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about several different things in your life, like work, relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities, even when most people might not think those things are a big deal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I don’t notice it that way.\n\nLike, work stuff, yes, that gets in my head. But relationships or health or, I don’t know, paying the electric bill—those don’t usually spiral the same way. So… I don’t know. It feels more centered around certain situations, not everything in my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even during routine or simple tasks, like running errands, watching TV, or walking around your neighborhood, even when there’s nothing stressful happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Not constantly like I’m panicking, but I’m kind of scanning without meaning to.\n\nLike on the train I’ll notice where the doors are, who’s standing too close, that kind of thing. And in meetings, even normal boring ones, I’ll catch myself checking the exits or listening to noises in the hallway instead of actually following what someone is saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because sometimes I’m just at lunch with my team, and they’re talking about, I don’t know, someone’s vacation or what they ordered, and I’m half paying attention to who’s walking behind us. It’s not that anything is happening. I just… my brain does it automatically.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety tends to follow you from one situation to another, even when the circumstances change—like from work to being at home, or from a meeting to a casual conversation—or does it usually fade once you leave a particular setting?\n\nPatient: It follows me, yeah. I wish it stayed in one place, like if I could just leave it at work when I badge out, but it’s not really like that.\n\nI’ll be on the train after work and I’m still clocking where the doors are, who’s standing too close, all of that. Then in meetings, even if nothing is happening, I’m half listening for hallway noises or checking where the exits are. And then even at lunch with my team, when it’s supposed to be casual, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of actually being in the conversation.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I used to be the person who could read on the train. Like actual books, not just scrolling. Now I’ll reread the same paragraph three times because I’m keeping track of everyone around me. So… yeah, it doesn’t exactly fade just because the setting changes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I think so, but it’s hard to separate because I don’t always notice it until after.

Like I’ve said, it’s definitely on the train and in meetings—my brain just starts clocking exits, who’s too close, little noises in the hallway. And even at lunch with my team, which should be nothing, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of listening to the conversation.

At home… I don’t know, I was trying to watch this show the other night and then I got distracted thinking about whether I’d answered an email, which is not what you asked—sorry. But the main thing is it’s not just “work stress.” It’s like my attention keeps getting pulled to whether things around me are safe or… I don’t know, controlled. Even when nothing is actually happening.

  {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Posttraumatic Stress Disorder. manifestation: I remember hiding and I remember the police being there, but there’s this blank section around when they actually came into the conference room and got me out, like the file is  manifestation: I’m here because I can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore and that I’m somehow damaged now, like the competent person who got promoted isn’t really there anymore.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking that if I had insisted on better security for the overnight deployment or called sooner, the guard might not have been hurt so badly, even though part of or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: In code reviews I snap at people over small things, like a sloppy rollback plan or someone questioning my timeline, and then I hear myself sounding harsh and gotten called out in a performance review for conflicts that never used to happen. I’ve also pulled back from mentoring and women-in-tech leadership events because I don’t feel steady or reliable there anymore.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the break-in during that overnight deployment about 2 years and 4 months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 28 / computer_systems_analyst / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S072\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even during routine or simple tasks, like running errands, watching TV, or walking around your neighborhood, even when there’s nothing stressful happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. And even at lunch with my team, which should be nothing, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of listening to the conversation.\n\nAt home… I don’t know, I was trying to watch this show the other night and then I got distracted thinking about whether I’d answered an email, which is not what you asked—sorry. I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly.

I still like things, I think. Like if the food is good, I know it’s good. Or if something funny happens in a show, I’ll laugh sometimes. But I also get distracted, and then I’m not really in it. That might be a different thing than not enjoying it, though.

On the train I’m clocking the exits before I even sit down. In meetings I notice who’s standing too close, or where the door is, and then I’m not really listening the way I should be. Even at lunch with my team, which should be totally normal, I’ll be half in the conversation and half listening to noises in the hallway.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I missed what someone said, and I feel awkward, like I’m being rude, but I can’t just… tell my brain to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop or control your anxious thoughts, even when you’re aware that they might not make sense or aren’t based on anything real?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, I can’t just switch it off.\n\nLike on the train, I’ll be thinking about something totally normal—what I need to do at work, or whether I forgot to reply to an email—and then suddenly I’ve already checked where the exits are. Then I get frustrated with myself because I know there’s nothing real happening, but my brain keeps doing it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying or feeling anxious about a variety of things in your life, not just work—like everyday responsibilities, your health, or things happening in your personal life—even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Everyday stuff like bills or groceries or calling to make an appointment—I can get annoyed or put it off, but I don’t know if I’d call it worry exactly.\n\nAlthough, now that you say “variety,” I’m thinking about how I do overthink little things sometimes, like whether I sounded weird in a text, but then again I’ve always done that a bit. My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. Even at lunch with my team, which should be totally normal, I’ll be half in the conversation and half listening to noises in the hallway.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I missed what someone said, and I feel awkward, like I’m being rude, but I can’t just… tell my brain to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop or control your anxious thoughts, even when you’re aware that they might not make sense or aren’t based on anything real?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, I can’t just switch it off.\n\nLike on the train, I’ll be thinking about something totally normal—what I need to do at work, or whether I forgot to reply to an email—and then suddenly I’ve already checked where the exits are. Or in meetings, I’m supposed to be listening, but I’m noticing who’s standing too close to me or where people are in the room.\n\nAnd lunch with my team is probably the best example because it’s not a threatening situation at all. It’s just people talking about weekend plans or whatever, and I still have one ear on the hallway noises instead of being fully in the conversation. Then I get frustrated with myself because I know there’s nothing real happening, but my brain keeps doing it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying or feeling anxious about a variety of things in your life, not just work—like everyday responsibilities, your health, or things happening in your personal life—even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… not in the same way, I don’t think.\n\nWork is the thing my mind keeps coming back to, or situations that feel like they connect to that somehow. Everyday stuff like bills or groceries or calling to make an appointment—I can get annoyed or put it off, but I don’t know if I’d call it worry exactly.\n\nAlthough, now that you say “variety,” I’m thinking about how I do overthink little things sometimes, like whether I sounded weird in a text, but then again I’ve always done that a bit. My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. It feels more specific than that, but I’m not sure I’m explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or difficulty concentrating that come along with these anxious feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly sleep, I think. I mean, concentration too, maybe, but the sleep is the thing I notice because then the next day everything feels harder.\n\nMost nights I’m lying there replaying these tiny details from work, like what someone’s face looked like in a meeting or whether I missed something important. And then sometimes I’ll jolt awake from dreams where I’m hiding under the conference table again, which sounds— I don’t know, it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in the moment.\n\nEven if I technically get six or seven hours, I wake up feeling like I never actually powered down. Like my body was lying there, but my brain was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about several different things in your life, like work, relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities, even when most people might not think those things are a big deal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I don’t notice it that way.\n\nLike, work stuff, yes, that gets in my head. But relationships or health or, I don’t know, paying the electric bill—those don’t usually spiral the same way. I can stress about them like anyone would, I guess.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one time I spent half a day worrying about a weird noise my car was making, but that was because it actually sounded expensive, not just out of nowhere. So… I don’t know. It feels more centered around certain situations, not everything in my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even during routine or simple tasks, like running errands, watching TV, or walking around your neighborhood, even when there’s nothing stressful happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Not constantly like I’m panicking, but I’m kind of scanning without meaning to.\n\nLike on the train I’ll notice where the doors are, who’s standing too close, that kind of thing. And in meetings, even normal boring ones, I’ll catch myself checking the exits or listening to noises in the hallway instead of actually following what someone is saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because sometimes I’m just at lunch with my team, and they’re talking about, I don’t know, someone’s vacation or what they ordered, and I’m half paying attention to who’s walking behind us. It’s not that anything is happening. I just… my brain does it automatically.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety tends to follow you from one situation to another, even when the circumstances change—like from work to being at home, or from a meeting to a casual conversation—or does it usually fade once you leave a particular setting?\n\nPatient: It follows me, yeah. I wish it stayed in one place, like if I could just leave it at work when I badge out, but it’s not really like that.\n\nI’ll be on the train after work and I’m still clocking where the doors are, who’s standing too close, all of that. Then in meetings, even if nothing is happening, I’m half listening for hallway noises or checking where the exits are. And then even at lunch with my team, when it’s supposed to be casual, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of actually being in the conversation.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I used to be the person who could read on the train. Like actual books, not just scrolling. Now I’ll reread the same paragraph three times because I’m keeping track of everyone around me. So… yeah, it doesn’t exactly fade just because the setting changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge in a wide range of everyday situations, not just at work or on the train, but even during simple activities like being at home, watching TV, or going for a walk, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so, but it’s hard to separate because I don’t always notice it until after.\n\nLike I’ve said, it’s definitely on the train and in meetings—my brain just starts clocking exits, who’s too close, little noises in the hallway. And even at lunch with my team, which should be nothing, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of listening to the conversation.\n\nAt home… I don’t know, I was trying to watch this show the other night and then I got distracted thinking about whether I’d answered an email, which is not what you asked—sorry. But the main thing is it’s not just “work stress.” It’s like my attention keeps getting pulled to whether things around me are safe or… I don’t know, controlled. Even when nothing is actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even small things, like a favorite show, a good meal, or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI still like things, I think. Like if the food is good, I know it’s good. Or if something funny happens in a show, I’ll laugh sometimes. But I also get distracted, and then I’m not really in it. That might be a different thing than not enjoying it, though.\n\nWith friends, I haven’t really—sorry, I’m trying not to over-explain. I guess I haven’t sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more like sometimes I’m there but not fully there. Does that count, or do you mean like feeling nothing from it at all?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Everyday stuff like bills or groceries or calling to make an appointment—I can get annoyed or put it off, but I don’t know if I’d call it worry exactly.\n\nAlthough, now that you say “variety,” I’m thinking about how I do overthink little things sometimes, like whether I sounded weird in a text, but then again I’ve always done that a bit. My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. It feels more specific than that, but I’m not sure I’m explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or difficulty concentrating that come along with these anxious feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly sleep, I think. I mean, concentration too, maybe, but the sleep is the thing I notice because then the next day everything feels harder.\n\nMost nights I’m lying there replaying these tiny details from work, like what someone’s face looked like in a meeting or whether I missed something important. And then sometimes I’ll jolt awake from dreams where I’m hiding under the conference table again, which sounds— I don’t know, it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in the moment.\n\nEven if I technically get six or seven hours, I wake up feeling like I never actually powered down. Like my body was lying there, but my brain was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about several different things in your life, like work, relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities, even when most people might not think those things are a big deal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I don’t notice it that way.\n\nLike, work stuff, yes, that gets in my head. But relationships or health or, I don’t know, paying the electric bill—those don’t usually spiral the same way. I can stress about them like anyone would, I guess.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one time I spent half a day worrying about a weird noise my car was making, but that was because it actually sounded expensive, not just out of nowhere. So… I don’t know. It feels more centered around certain situations, not everything in my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even during routine or simple tasks, like running errands, watching TV, or walking around your neighborhood, even when there’s nothing stressful happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Not constantly like I’m panicking, but I’m kind of scanning without meaning to.\n\nLike on the train I’ll notice where the doors are, who’s standing too close, that kind of thing. And in meetings, even normal boring ones, I’ll catch myself checking the exits or listening to noises in the hallway instead of actually following what someone is saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because sometimes I’m just at lunch with my team, and they’re talking about, I don’t know, someone’s vacation or what they ordered, and I’m half paying attention to who’s walking behind us. It’s not that anything is happening. I just… my brain does it automatically.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety tends to follow you from one situation to another, even when the circumstances change—like from work to being at home, or from a meeting to a casual conversation—or does it usually fade once you leave a particular setting?\n\nPatient: It follows me, yeah. I wish it stayed in one place, like if I could just leave it at work when I badge out, but it’s not really like that.\n\nI’ll be on the train after work and I’m still clocking where the doors are, who’s standing too close, all of that. Then in meetings, even if nothing is happening, I’m half listening for hallway noises or checking where the exits are. And then even at lunch with my team, when it’s supposed to be casual, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of actually being in the conversation.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I used to be the person who could read on the train. Like actual books, not just scrolling. Now I’ll reread the same paragraph three times because I’m keeping track of everyone around me. So… yeah, it doesn’t exactly fade just because the setting changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge in a wide range of everyday situations, not just at work or on the train, but even during simple activities like being at home, watching TV, or going for a walk, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so, but it’s hard to separate because I don’t always notice it until after.\n\nLike I’ve said, it’s definitely on the train and in meetings—my brain just starts clocking exits, who’s too close, little noises in the hallway. And even at lunch with my team, which should be nothing, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of listening to the conversation.\n\nAt home… I don’t know, I was trying to watch this show the other night and then I got distracted thinking about whether I’d answered an email, which is not what you asked—sorry. But the main thing is it’s not just “work stress.” It’s like my attention keeps getting pulled to whether things around me are safe or… I don’t know, controlled. Even when nothing is actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even small things, like a favorite show, a good meal, or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI still like things, I think. Like if the food is good, I know it’s good. Or if something funny happens in a show, I’ll laugh sometimes. But I also get distracted, and then I’m not really in it. That might be a different thing than not enjoying it, though.\n\nWith friends, I haven’t really—sorry, I’m trying not to over-explain. I guess I haven’t sat down and thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more like sometimes I’m there but not fully there. Does that count, or do you mean like feeling nothing from it at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling sad or in a low mood for most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing particularly bad has happened?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I have bad stretches, but “low mood” feels… I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of flat or worn down, but then I’ll get annoyed about something stupid, like my apartment building keeps changing the package room code and nobody tells you, and then I’m focused on that instead. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been tracking it like that. I don’t wake up every day thinking, “I’m sad.” It’s more like I notice I’m not quite myself, but I’m not sure if that’s sadness exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And even at lunch with my team, which should be nothing, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of listening to the conversation.\n\nAt home… I don’t know, I was trying to watch this show the other night and then I got distracted thinking about whether I’d answered an email, which is not what you asked—sorry. But the main thing is it’s not just “work stress.” It’s like my attention keeps getting pulled to whether things around me are safe or… I don’t know, controlled. Even when nothing is actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even small things, like a favorite show, a good meal, or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI still like things, I think. I mean, I have bad stretches, but “low mood” feels… I’m not sure.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of flat or worn down, but then I’ll get annoyed about something stupid, like my apartment building keeps changing the package room code and nobody tells you, and then I’m focused on that instead. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been tracking it like that. I don’t wake up every day thinking, “I’m sad.” It’s more like I notice I’m not quite myself, but I’m not sure if that’s sadness exactly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your anxiety is present even in situations where nothing stressful or threatening is happening, or does it usually only come up in specific situations that remind you of something unsettling?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 28 / computer_systems_analyst / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hypervigilance\nmanifestation: \"On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I automatically clock the exits, watch who is standing too close, and keep one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of being fully present.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more tied to certain situations, but they’re not always “stressful” in the normal sense. Like the train, sure, that makes sense to me. But meetings? Lunch with my team? Those aren’t supposed to be threatening.

And still, I’ll catch myself automatically checking where the exits are, noticing who’s standing too close, or keeping one ear on noises in the hallway instead of actually paying attention. It’s like my brain is doing this safety scan in the background.

Sorry, I’m kind of circling it. It’s not like every quiet moment at home, but it does come up in these everyday places where nothing is actually happening.

Can you tell me what’s been bringing you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I don’t feel like work is safe anymore. Which sounds strange, because it’s just an office, computers, meetings, all that. I used to be really good at it—I got promoted, people trusted me—and now I keep feeling like that version of me isn’t really there.\n\nLike I’m damaged somehow. And then I start thinking about totally unrelated things, like how I used to stay late debugging stuff and feel proud of it, and now even thinking about going in makes me feel like something is wrong with me. I don’t know. I just can’t shake that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in how much you enjoy things you used to like, such as work or hobbies?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… work is complicated because it’s not like I ever “enjoyed” every meeting or spreadsheet, you know? Sometimes I liked solving problems, especially when something finally worked after hours of staring at it.\n\nHobbies… I don’t know. I used to have little routines, like making coffee a certain way before logging in, or reading tech forums even when I didn’t need to. But then I start thinking, was that enjoyment or just habit? Sorry, I’m not answering very clearly. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not always like I’m worrying about a specific thing, like a deadline or an email. It’s more like I’m scanning everything.\n\nOn the train I’ll notice where the exits are before I even sit down, and in meetings I’m watching who’s near the door or who’s standing too close. Even at lunch with my team, when we’re supposed to just be talking about whatever—someone’s dog, or this place near the office that has really good noodles—I’m still half-listening to noises in the hallway instead of the conversation.\n\nSo… yes, anxious, I guess. But it feels more like being on alert all the time than normal worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling this way even in places or situations that you know are completely safe, like at home or with close friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it happens even when there’s no real reason. Like on the train, I’ll automatically look for the exits before I’ve even really thought about it. And at work, in meetings, I’m watching who’s standing too close or where people are in the room.\n\nEven at lunch with my team—when it’s just normal conversation—I’ll have one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of actually being fully there. It’s weird because I know it’s supposed to be safe, like it’s just lunch, but my brain doesn’t seem to believe that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or like you’re not worth much, most days—even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “down” isn’t always the word I would use. It’s more like this feeling that something changed in me and I can’t get back to who I was.\n\nLike, I got promoted before all this, and people at work saw me as competent, the person who could handle problems. And now I keep thinking work isn’t safe anymore, even when I know that sounds dramatic. Then I start wondering if I’m… damaged somehow. Like the version of me who could just do the job and trust herself isn’t really there now.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling around it. I don’t know if that’s hopeless exactly, but it does make me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop your worrying, even when you know there isn’t really anything specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s hard to shut off, even when I know there isn’t one clear thing I’m supposed to be worried about.\n\nIt’s like my brain keeps checking anyway. On the train I’m clocking the exits before I even sit down. In meetings I notice who’s standing too close, or where the door is, and then I’m not really listening the way I should be. Even at lunch with my team, which should be totally normal, I’ll be half in the conversation and half listening to noises in the hallway.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I missed what someone said, and I feel awkward, like I’m being rude, but I can’t just… tell my brain to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop or control your anxious thoughts, even when you’re aware that they might not make sense or aren’t based on anything real?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, I can’t just switch it off.\n\nLike on the train, I’ll be thinking about something totally normal—what I need to do at work, or whether I forgot to reply to an email—and then suddenly I’ve already checked where the exits are. Or in meetings, I’m supposed to be listening, but I’m noticing who’s standing too close to me or where people are in the room.\n\nAnd lunch with my team is probably the best example because it’s not a threatening situation at all. It’s just people talking about weekend plans or whatever, and I still have one ear on the hallway noises instead of being fully in the conversation. Then I get frustrated with myself because I know there’s nothing real happening, but my brain keeps doing it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying or feeling anxious about a variety of things in your life, not just work—like everyday responsibilities, your health, or things happening in your personal life—even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… not in the same way, I don’t think.\n\nWork is the thing my mind keeps coming back to, or situations that feel like they connect to that somehow. Everyday stuff like bills or groceries or calling to make an appointment—I can get annoyed or put it off, but I don’t know if I’d call it worry exactly.\n\nAlthough, now that you say “variety,” I’m thinking about how I do overthink little things sometimes, like whether I sounded weird in a text, but then again I’ve always done that a bit. My roommate used to tease me in grad school because I’d rewrite emails like five times, which is probably not what you’re asking.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed myself worrying across everything. It feels more specific than that, but I’m not sure I’m explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or difficulty concentrating that come along with these anxious feelings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly sleep, I think. I mean, concentration too, maybe, but the sleep is the thing I notice because then the next day everything feels harder.\n\nMost nights I’m lying there replaying these tiny details from work, like what someone’s face looked like in a meeting or whether I missed something important. And then sometimes I’ll jolt awake from dreams where I’m hiding under the conference table again, which sounds— I don’t know, it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but it feels really real in the moment.\n\nEven if I technically get six or seven hours, I wake up feeling like I never actually powered down. Like my body was lying there, but my brain was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about several different things in your life, like work, relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities, even when most people might not think those things are a big deal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I don’t notice it that way.\n\nLike, work stuff, yes, that gets in my head. But relationships or health or, I don’t know, paying the electric bill—those don’t usually spiral the same way. I can stress about them like anyone would, I guess.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one time I spent half a day worrying about a weird noise my car was making, but that was because it actually sounded expensive, not just out of nowhere. So… I don’t know. It feels more centered around certain situations, not everything in my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even during routine or simple tasks, like running errands, watching TV, or walking around your neighborhood, even when there’s nothing stressful happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Not constantly like I’m panicking, but I’m kind of scanning without meaning to.\n\nLike on the train I’ll notice where the doors are, who’s standing too close, that kind of thing. And in meetings, even normal boring ones, I’ll catch myself checking the exits or listening to noises in the hallway instead of actually following what someone is saying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because sometimes I’m just at lunch with my team, and they’re talking about, I don’t know, someone’s vacation or what they ordered, and I’m half paying attention to who’s walking behind us. It’s not that anything is happening. I just… my brain does it automatically.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety tends to follow you from one situation to another, even when the circumstances change—like from work to being at home, or from a meeting to a casual conversation—or does it usually fade once you leave a particular setting?\n\nPatient: It follows me, yeah. I wish it stayed in one place, like if I could just leave it at work when I badge out, but it’s not really like that.\n\nI’ll be on the train after work and I’m still clocking where the doors are, who’s standing too close, all of that. Then in meetings, even if nothing is happening, I’m half listening for hallway noises or checking where the exits are. And then even at lunch with my team, when it’s supposed to be casual, I’ll realize I’m watching people move around instead of actually being in the conversation.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I used to be the person who could read on the train. Like actual books, not just scrolling. Now I’ll reread the same paragraph three times because I’m keeping track of everyone around me. So… yeah, it doesn’t exactly fade just because the setting changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge in a wide range of everyday situations, not just at work or on the train, but even during simple activities like being at home, watching TV, or going for a walk, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so, but it’s hard to separate because I don’t always notice it until after.\n\nLike I’ve said, it’s definitely on the train and in meetings—my brain just starts clocking exits, who’s too close, little noises in the hallway.
